name: Listening_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n  manifestation: During architecture meetings, someone will ask me a direct question and I realize I caught the first few words but then drifted into thinking about an implementation detail, so I have to ask them to say it again.\n- key: S005\n  name: Organization_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n  manifestation: What finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize the moving parts anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I end up starting whatever is loudest instead of what actually matters.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly hunting for things I need to work, like my USB-C adapters, stylus, sketchbook, or even my badge, and it can derail a whole morning because I have no memory What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I’m having a harder time organizing all the moving parts. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that well, but it feels like I’m losing the ability to keep it all coordinated.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that you feel you can't control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, one thought looping that I can’t stop. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that well, but it feels like I’m losing the ability to keep it all coordinated.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that you feel you can't control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, one thought looping that I can’t stop. Yeah… I do, especially if it’s something that has to stay in my head continuously, like coding through a complicated section. I can be focused, but it’s kind of fragile. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound worse than it is, because I am still getting things done, mostly. But what finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore.

Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I’m having a harder time organizing all the moving parts. It’s probably not catastrophic, but it’s becoming a problem.\n\nI’ve got patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—just a lot of separate things—and instead of sorting out what actually matters, I keep starting whatever feels most urgent or loudest in the moment. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that well, but it feels like I’m losing the ability to keep it all coordinated.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that you feel you can't control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, one thought looping that I can’t stop. I mean, I’ll get stuck thinking about work things sometimes, but I think that’s pretty normal? It’s more that I lose track of what I should focus on, rather than feeling like a thought is controlling me. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say often, no. I mean, I get tense when there’s a lot to manage, but I don’t think I’m walking around anxious all the time or anything like that. It feels more situational, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, even when you don't think it's necessary?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll double-check things occasionally, like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m doing repeated checking or cleaning when it doesn’t make sense. At least nothing that’s stood out to me as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not often. I mean, I’m sure I do small things without fully thinking them through sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m, like, reckless or constantly acting on impulse. If anything, I usually try to be pretty careful. Does that fit what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to meet your responsibilities or maintain your relationships because of these organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’d like to say I’m mostly keeping up, but it has started to affect how people see me at work. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which was embarrassing, and then a pretty difficult architecture review didn’t go well. Since then I’ve gotten the sense that some collaborators are wondering whether I can really lead the larger project.\n\nI don’t think it’s, you know, catastrophic, but it’s not nothing either. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: A bit, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but on long design calls I notice this pressure building up in my body, like I need to move. I’ll pace around my office or switch chairs a few times, even while I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially if it’s something that has to stay in my head continuously, like coding through a complicated section. I can be focused, but it’s kind of fragile. If Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some random visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread pretty quickly.\n\nAnd then, before I really notice it, I’ve spent twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole instead of what I was supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as “hard to focus for a long time,” but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to keep track of your tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound worse than it is, because I am still getting things done, mostly. But what finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore.\n\nThere are patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions… and instead of calmly sorting out what matters most, I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment. Then I realize later I’ve let something more important slip, and that’s when I get pretty stressed.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by overwhelmed, but it feels like I’m constantly trying to catch up with my own responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships outside of work due to your organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m neglecting people on purpose or anything. It’s more that the same organizing problem spills over.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts straight anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I end up starting whatever feels loudest instead of what actually matters. So outside of work, I can be slower to follow through or I’ll realize I haven’t planned something I meant to.\n\nI guess it probably does affect relationships some, even if I try to cover for it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or overlooking details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.\n\nIn code reviews, for example, I’ll sometimes miss something embarrassingly small, like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file. And then later, when someone points it out or I look back, it’s right there. It’s not complicated — I just skimmed past it somehow.\n\nSo yes, I guess that does happen. I’m not sure how often counts as “often,” but enough that it bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do get upset, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s small, like, okay, it’s just a code review comment, not the end of the world.

But when I miss something really obvious — like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file — and then I look back and it was right there in front of me, I feel embarrassed. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I didn’t understand it. I just somehow skimmed past it.

So yes, it can be draining. Maybe not every single time, but enough that it sticks with me. Does that make sense?

I mean, I’m sure I do small things without fully thinking them through sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m, like, reckless or constantly acting on impulse. If anything, I usually try to be pretty careful. Does that fit what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to meet your responsibilities or maintain your relationships because of these organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’d like to say I’m mostly keeping up, but it has started to affect how people see me at work. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which was embarrassing, and then a pretty difficult architecture review didn’t go well. Since then I’ve gotten the sense that some collaborators are wondering whether I can really lead the larger project.\n\nI don’t think it’s, you know, catastrophic, but it’s not nothing either. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: A bit, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but on long design calls I notice this pressure building up in my body, like I need to move. I’ll pace around my office or switch chairs a few times, even while I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially if it’s something that has to stay in my head continuously, like coding through a complicated section. I can be focused, but it’s kind of fragile. If Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some random visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread pretty quickly.\n\nAnd then, before I really notice it, I’ve spent twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole instead of what I was supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as “hard to focus for a long time,” but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to keep track of your tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound worse than it is, because I am still getting things done, mostly. But what finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore.\n\nThere are patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions… and instead of calmly sorting out what matters most, I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment. Then I realize later I’ve let something more important slip, and that’s when I get pretty stressed.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by overwhelmed, but it feels like I’m constantly trying to catch up with my own responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships outside of work due to your organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m neglecting people on purpose or anything. It’s more that the same organizing problem spills over.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts straight anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I end up starting whatever feels loudest instead of what actually matters. So outside of work, I can be slower to follow through or I’ll realize I haven’t planned something I meant to.\n\nI guess it probably does affect relationships some, even if I try to cover for it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or overlooking details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.\n\nIn code reviews, for example, I’ll sometimes miss something embarrassingly small, like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file. And then later, when someone points it out or I look back, it’s right there. It’s not complicated — I just skimmed past it somehow.\n\nSo yes, I guess that does happen. I’m not sure how often counts as “often,” but enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or emotionally drained when you make these careless mistakes or overlook details?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get upset, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s small, like, okay, it’s just a code review comment, not the end of the world.\n\nBut when I miss something really obvious — like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file — and then I look back and it was right there in front of me, I feel embarrassed. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I didn’t understand it. I just somehow skimmed past it.\n\nSo yes, it can be draining. Maybe not every single time, but enough that it sticks with me. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. If Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some random visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread pretty quickly.\n\nAnd then, before I really notice it, I’ve spent twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole instead of what I was supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as “hard to focus for a long time,” but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to keep track of your tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound worse than it is, because I am still getting things done, mostly. Then I realize later I’ve let something more important slip, and that’s when I get pretty stressed.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by overwhelmed, but it feels like I’m constantly trying to catch up with my own responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships outside of work due to your organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m neglecting people on purpose or anything. It’s more that the same organizing problem spills over.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts straight anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I end up starting whatever feels loudest instead of what actually matters. So outside of work, I can be slower to follow through or I’ll realize I haven’t planned something I meant to.\n\nI guess it probably does affect relationships some, even if I try to cover for it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or overlooking details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.\n\nIn code reviews, for example, I’ll sometimes miss something embarrassingly small, like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file. And then later, when someone points it out or I look back, it’s right there. It’s not complicated — I just skimmed past it somehow.\n\nSo yes, I guess that does happen. I’m not sure how often counts as “often,” but enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or emotionally drained when you make these careless mistakes or overlook details?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get upset, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s small, like, okay, it’s just a code review comment, not the end of the world.\n\nBut when I miss something really obvious — like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file — and then I look back and it was right there in front of me, I feel embarrassed. I don’t think it’s totally new, no. I mean, I’ve gotten better at covering for it as an adult, I think, because work kind of forces you to build systems.

I’ll pace around my office or switch chairs a few times, even while I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially if it’s something that has to stay in my head continuously, like coding through a complicated section. I can be focused, but it’s kind of fragile. If Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some random visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread pretty quickly.\n\nAnd then, before I really notice it, I’ve spent twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole instead of what I was supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as “hard to focus for a long time,” but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to keep track of your tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound worse than it is, because I am still getting things done, mostly. But what finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore.\n\nThere are patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions… and instead of calmly sorting out what matters most, I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment. Then I realize later I’ve let something more important slip, and that’s when I get pretty stressed.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by overwhelmed, but it feels like I’m constantly trying to catch up with my own responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships outside of work due to your organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m neglecting people on purpose or anything. It’s more that the same organizing problem spills over.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts straight anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I end up starting whatever feels loudest instead of what actually matters. So outside of work, I can be slower to follow through or I’ll realize I haven’t planned something I meant to.\n\nI guess it probably does affect relationships some, even if I try to cover for it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or overlooking details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.\n\nIn code reviews, for example, I’ll sometimes miss something embarrassingly small, like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file. And then later, when someone points it out or I look back, it’s right there. It’s not complicated — I just skimmed past it somehow.\n\nSo yes, I guess that does happen. I’m not sure how often counts as “often,” but enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or emotionally drained when you make these careless mistakes or overlook details?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get upset, probably more than I’d like to admit. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I didn’t understand it. I just somehow skimmed past it.\n\nSo yes, it can be draining. Maybe not every single time, but enough that it sticks with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn or interrupt others during conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me, honestly. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say often, no. I mean, I get tense when there’s a lot to manage, but I don’t think I’m walking around anxious all the time or anything like that. It feels more situational, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, even when you don't think it's necessary?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll double-check things occasionally, like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m doing repeated checking or cleaning when it doesn’t make sense. At least nothing that’s stood out to me as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not often. I mean, I’m sure I do small things without fully thinking them through sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m, like, reckless or constantly acting on impulse. If anything, I usually try to be pretty careful. Does that fit what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to meet your responsibilities or maintain your relationships because of these organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’d like to say I’m mostly keeping up, but it has started to affect how people see me at work. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which was embarrassing, and then a pretty difficult architecture review didn’t go well. Since then I’ve gotten the sense that some collaborators are wondering whether I can really lead the larger project.\n\nI don’t think it’s, you know, catastrophic, but it’s not nothing either. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: A bit, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but on long design calls I notice this pressure building up in my body, like I need to move. I’ll pace around my office or switch chairs a few times, even while I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially if it’s something that has to stay in my head continuously, like coding through a complicated section. I can be focused, but it’s kind of fragile. If Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some random visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread pretty quickly.\n\nAnd then, before I really notice it, I’ve spent twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole instead of what I was supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as “hard to focus for a long time,” but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to keep track of your tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound worse than it is, because I am still getting things done, mostly. But what finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore.\n\nThere are patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions… and instead of calmly sorting out what matters most, I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment. Then I realize later I’ve let something more important slip, and that’s when I get pretty stressed.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by overwhelmed, but it feels like I’m constantly trying to catch up with my own responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships outside of work due to your organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m neglecting people on purpose or anything. It’s more that the same organizing problem spills over.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts straight anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I end up starting whatever feels loudest instead of what actually matters. So outside of work, I can be slower to follow through or I’ll realize I haven’t planned something I meant to.\n\nI guess it probably does affect relationships some, even if I try to cover for it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or overlooking details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.\n\nIn code reviews, for example, I’ll sometimes miss something embarrassingly small, like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file. And then later, when someone points it out or I look back, it’s right there. It’s not complicated — I just skimmed past it somehow.\n\nSo yes, I guess that does happen. I’m not sure how often counts as “often,” but enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or emotionally drained when you make these careless mistakes or overlook details?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get upset, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s small, like, okay, it’s just a code review comment, not the end of the world.\n\nBut when I miss something really obvious — like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file — and then I look back and it was right there in front of me, I feel embarrassed. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I didn’t understand it. I just somehow skimmed past it.\n\nSo yes, it can be draining. Maybe not every single time, but enough that it sticks with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn or interrupt others during conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me, honestly. I mean, I’m sure I’ve interrupted people here and there like anyone does, especially if I’m trying not to lose a thought, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly cutting people off or unable to wait my turn.\n\nIn conversations, I’m usually more likely to hang back a bit or try to make sure I’m not being rude. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of organizational and focus challenges at other times in your life, or is this a relatively new development for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s totally new, no. I mean, I’ve gotten better at covering for it as an adult, I think, because work kind of forces you to build systems.\n\nBut looking back, it was there pretty early. In grade school, teachers would write things like that I was bright, but I daydreamed, or that I didn’t turn things in. And sometimes I actually had finished the homework, but I’d forget to hand it in or I’d lose the folder.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but yeah, this pattern was there well before I was a teenager. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have these organizational and focus challenges caused you significant distress or impaired your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, although I’m a little uncomfortable saying that because I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job.\n\nBut yes, it’s started to affect how people see me at work. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, which was… not great. And there was a pretty difficult architecture review where I don’t think I was as on top of the details as I needed to be. After that, I got the sense that some collaborators were questioning whether I should really be leading the larger project.\n\nSo, I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart, but it has gone beyond just being annoying or embarrassing. It’s affecting my reputation and confidence at work. Is that the kind of impairment you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes, it’s both, though I think I notice it more at work because the stakes are clearer.

I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but on long design calls I notice this pressure building up in my body, like I need to move. I’ll pace around my office or switch chairs a few times, even while I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially if it’s something that has to stay in my head continuously, like coding through a complicated section. I can be focused, but it’s kind of fragile. If Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some random visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread pretty quickly.\n\nAnd then, before I really notice it, I’ve spent twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole instead of what I was supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as “hard to focus for a long time,” but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to keep track of your tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound worse than it is, because I am still getting things done, mostly. But what finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore.\n\nThere are patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions… and instead of calmly sorting out what matters most, I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment. Then I realize later I’ve let something more important slip, and that’s when I get pretty stressed.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by overwhelmed, but it feels like I’m constantly trying to catch up with my own responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships outside of work due to your organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m neglecting people on purpose or anything. It’s more that the same organizing problem spills over.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts straight anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I end up starting whatever feels loudest instead of what actually matters. So outside of work, I can be slower to follow through or I’ll realize I haven’t planned something I meant to.\n\nI guess it probably does affect relationships some, even if I try to cover for it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or overlooking details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.\n\nIn code reviews, for example, I’ll sometimes miss something embarrassingly small, like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file. And then later, when someone points it out or I look back, it’s right there. It’s not complicated — I just skimmed past it somehow.\n\nSo yes, I guess that does happen. I’m not sure how often counts as “often,” but enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or emotionally drained when you make these careless mistakes or overlook details?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get upset, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s small, like, okay, it’s just a code review comment, not the end of the world.\n\nBut when I miss something really obvious — like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file — and then I look back and it was right there in front of me, I feel embarrassed. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I didn’t understand it. I just somehow skimmed past it.\n\nSo yes, it can be draining. Maybe not every single time, but enough that it sticks with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn or interrupt others during conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me, honestly. I mean, I’m sure I’ve interrupted people here and there like anyone does, especially if I’m trying not to lose a thought, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly cutting people off or unable to wait my turn.\n\nIn conversations, I’m usually more likely to hang back a bit or try to make sure I’m not being rude. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of organizational and focus challenges at other times in your life, or is this a relatively new development for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s totally new, no. I mean, I’ve gotten better at covering for it as an adult, I think, because work kind of forces you to build systems.\n\nBut looking back, it was there pretty early. In grade school, teachers would write things like that I was bright, but I daydreamed, or that I didn’t turn things in. And sometimes I actually had finished the homework, but I’d forget to hand it in or I’d lose the folder.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but yeah, this pattern was there well before I was a teenager. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have these organizational and focus challenges caused you significant distress or impaired your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, although I’m a little uncomfortable saying that because I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job.\n\nBut yes, it’s started to affect how people see me at work. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, which was… not great. And there was a pretty difficult architecture review where I don’t think I was as on top of the details as I needed to be. After that, I got the sense that some collaborators were questioning whether I should really be leading the larger project.\n\nSo, I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart, but it has gone beyond just being annoying or embarrassing. It’s affecting my reputation and confidence at work. Is that the kind of impairment you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and focus are the main problems you're experiencing, or do you also struggle with feeling restless or impulsive in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’d say the organization and focus are the main things, yes.\n\nI don’t think of myself as especially impulsive, at least not in the way people usually mean. But… I do get physically restless sometimes, especially on long design calls. I’ll feel this sort of pressure building, like I need to get up, and then I’ll pace around my office or move from one chair to another while I’m still trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive or anything. I usually keep it off-camera if I can. But yes, there is some restlessness there. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these organizational and focus challenges both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s both, though I think I notice it more at work because the stakes are clearer.\n\nAt home it’s similar in the sense that there are just… a lot of moving parts, and I don’t seem to organize them very well. What finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to keep track of everything anymore — patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions — and I end up doing whatever feels most urgent or loudest, instead of what actually matters most.\n\nI’m not trying to be careless. I usually have good intentions and I do make lists, but the pieces don’t seem to stay organized in my head. Does that answer what you mean by at work and at home?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Does that answer what you mean by at work and at home?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been diagnosed with or treated for ADHD?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / software_developer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: A bit, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but on long design calls I notice this pressure building up in my body, like I need to move. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially if it’s something that has to stay in my head continuously, like coding through a complicated section. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or overlooking details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.\n\nIn code reviews, for example, I’ll sometimes miss something embarrassingly small, like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file. And then later, when someone points it out or I look back, it’s right there. It’s not complicated — I just skimmed past it somehow.\n\nSo yes, I guess that does happen. I’m not sure how often counts as “often,” but enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or emotionally drained when you make these careless mistakes or overlook details?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get upset, probably more than I’d like to admit. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s been there in some form for a long time, yes. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, because I did well enough in school overall, but looking back, there were signs even in grade school.

Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: A bit, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but on long design calls I notice this pressure building up in my body, like I need to move. I’ll pace around my office or switch chairs a few times, even while I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially if it’s something that has to stay in my head continuously, like coding through a complicated section. I can be focused, but it’s kind of fragile. If Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some random visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread pretty quickly.\n\nAnd then, before I really notice it, I’ve spent twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole instead of what I was supposed to be doing. I don’t know if that counts as “hard to focus for a long time,” but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or stressed when you're unable to keep track of your tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound worse than it is, because I am still getting things done, mostly. But what finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore.\n\nThere are patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions… and instead of calmly sorting out what matters most, I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment. Then I realize later I’ve let something more important slip, and that’s when I get pretty stressed.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by overwhelmed, but it feels like I’m constantly trying to catch up with my own responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships outside of work due to your organizational challenges?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m neglecting people on purpose or anything. It’s more that the same organizing problem spills over.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts straight anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I end up starting whatever feels loudest instead of what actually matters. So outside of work, I can be slower to follow through or I’ll realize I haven’t planned something I meant to.\n\nI guess it probably does affect relationships some, even if I try to cover for it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or overlooking details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m careless exactly, but I do miss details that I really feel like I should have caught.\n\nIn code reviews, for example, I’ll sometimes miss something embarrassingly small, like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file. And then later, when someone points it out or I look back, it’s right there. It’s not complicated — I just skimmed past it somehow.\n\nSo yes, I guess that does happen. I’m not sure how often counts as “often,” but enough that it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or emotionally drained when you make these careless mistakes or overlook details?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get upset, probably more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s small, like, okay, it’s just a code review comment, not the end of the world.\n\nBut when I miss something really obvious — like a reversed condition or a version number in a dependency file — and then I look back and it was right there in front of me, I feel embarrassed. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I didn’t understand it. I just somehow skimmed past it.\n\nSo yes, it can be draining. Maybe not every single time, but enough that it sticks with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn or interrupt others during conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me, honestly. I mean, I’m sure I’ve interrupted people here and there like anyone does, especially if I’m trying not to lose a thought, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly cutting people off or unable to wait my turn.\n\nIn conversations, I’m usually more likely to hang back a bit or try to make sure I’m not being rude. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of organizational and focus challenges at other times in your life, or is this a relatively new development for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s totally new, no. I mean, I’ve gotten better at covering for it as an adult, I think, because work kind of forces you to build systems.\n\nBut looking back, it was there pretty early. In grade school, teachers would write things like that I was bright, but I daydreamed, or that I didn’t turn things in. And sometimes I actually had finished the homework, but I’d forget to hand it in or I’d lose the folder.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but yeah, this pattern was there well before I was a teenager. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have these organizational and focus challenges caused you significant distress or impaired your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, although I’m a little uncomfortable saying that because I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job.\n\nBut yes, it’s started to affect how people see me at work. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, which was… not great. And there was a pretty difficult architecture review where I don’t think I was as on top of the details as I needed to be. After that, I got the sense that some collaborators were questioning whether I should really be leading the larger project.\n\nSo, I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart, but it has gone beyond just being annoying or embarrassing. It’s affecting my reputation and confidence at work. Is that the kind of impairment you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and focus are the main problems you're experiencing, or do you also struggle with feeling restless or impulsive in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’d say the organization and focus are the main things, yes.\n\nI don’t think of myself as especially impulsive, at least not in the way people usually mean. I usually keep it off-camera if I can. But yes, there is some restlessness there. Does that fit with what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these organizational and focus challenges both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s both, though I think I notice it more at work because the stakes are clearer.\n\nAt home it’s similar in the sense that there are just… a lot of moving parts, and I don’t seem to organize them very well. I mean, I’m sure I’ve interrupted people here and there like anyone does, especially if I’m trying not to lose a thought, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly cutting people off or unable to wait my turn.\n\nIn conversations, I’m usually more likely to hang back a bit or try to make sure I’m not being rude. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of organizational and focus challenges at other times in your life, or is this a relatively new development for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s totally new, no. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have these organizational and focus challenges caused you significant distress or impaired your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, although I’m a little uncomfortable saying that because I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job.\n\nBut yes, it’s started to affect how people see me at work.
